Complete Multiparty Session Type Projection with Automata
Elaine Li, Felix Stutz *, Thomas Wies, Damien Zufferey

Abstract
Multiparty session types (MSTs) are a type-based approach to verifying communication protocols. Central to MSTs is a projection operator : a partial function that maps protocols represented as global types to correct-by-construction implementations for each participant, represented as a communicating state machine. Existing projection operators are syntactic in nature, and trade efficiency for completeness. We present the first projection operator that is sound, complete, and efficient. Our projection separates synthesis from checking implementability. For synthesis, we use a simple automata-theoretic construction; for checking implementability, we present succinct conditions that summarize insights into the property of implementability. We use these conditions to show that MST implementability is in PSPACE. This improves upon a previous decision procedure that is in EXPSPACE and applies to a smaller class of MSTs. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach using a prototype implementation, which handles global types not supported by previous work without sacrificing performance.



1
Introduction

Communication protocols are key components in many safety and operation critical systems, making them prime targets for formal verification.
Unfortunately, most verification problems for such protocols (e.g. deadlock freedom) are undecidable
[
10
]
.
To make verification computationally tractable, several restrictions have been proposed
[
13
,
9
,
3
,
2
,
30
,
40
]
.
In particular, multiparty session types (MSTs)
[
22
]
have garnered a lot of attention in recent years (see, e.g., the survey by Ancona et al.
[
5
]
).
In the MST setting, a protocol is specified as a global type, which describes the desired interactions of all roles involved in the protocol.
Local implementations describe behaviors for each individual role.
The implementability problem for a global type asks whether there exists a collection of local implementations whose composite behavior when viewed as a communicating state machine (CSM) matches that of the global type and is deadlock-free.
The synthesis problem is to compute such an implementation from an implementable global type.

MST-based approaches typically solve synthesis and implementability simultaneously via an efficient syntactic
projection operator

[
22
,
17
,
39
,
31
]
.
Abstractly, a
projection operator is a partial map from global types to collections of implementations.
A projection operator
proj
is sound when every global type
\mathbf{G}
in its domain is implemented by
\texttt{proj}(\mathbf{G})
, and complete when every implementable global type is in its domain.
Existing practical projection operators for MSTs are all incomplete (or unsound).
Recently, the implementability problem was shown to be decidable for a class of MSTs via a reduction to safe realizability of globally cooperative high-level message sequence charts (HMSCs)
[
36
]
. In principle, this result yields a complete and sound projection operator for the considered class. However, this operator would not be practical. In particular, the proposed implementability check is in
EXPSPACE
.

Contributions.

In this paper, we present the first practical sound and complete projection operator for general MSTs.
The synthesis problem for implementable global types is conceptually easy
[
36
]

– the challenge lies in determining whether a global type
is
implementable.
We thus separate synthesis from checking implementability.
We first use a standard automata-theoretic construction to obtain a candidate implementation for a potentially non-implementable global type.
However, unlike
[
36
]
, we then verify the correctness of this implementation directly using efficiently checkable conditions derived from the global type.
When a global type is not implementable, our constructive completeness proof provides a counterexample trace.

The resulting projection operator yields a PSPACE decision procedure for implementability.
1
1

1

A previous version of this paper claimed that the implementability problem for MSTs is PSPACE-hard, but there was an error in the proof. This version has since been edited to remove the claim. Instead, we show that there exist global types for which the construction of an implementation requires exponential time.

This result both generalizes and tightens the decidability and complexity results obtained in
[
36
]
.

We evaluate a prototype of our projection algorithm on benchmarks taken from the literature.
Our prototype benefits from both the efficiency of existing lightweight but incomplete syntactic projection operators
[
22
,
17
,
39
,
31
]
, and the generality of heavyweight automata-based model checking techniques
[
33
,
26
]
: it handles protocols rejected by previous practical approaches while preserving the efficiency that makes MST-based techniques so attractive.

Figure 1

Odd-even: An implementable but not (yet) projectable protocol and its local implementations

Incompleteness of existing projection operators.

A key limitation of existing projection operators is that the implementation for each role is obtained via a linear traversal of the global type, and thus shares its structure.
The following example, which is not projectable by any existing approach, demonstrates how enforcing structural similarity can lead to incompleteness.

Example 1
(Odd-even)

Consider the following global type
\mathbf{G}_{oe}
:

+\;\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,\mu t_{1}.\,({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,t_{1}\;+\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,0)\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,\mu t_{2}.\,({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,t_{2}\;+\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,0)\end{cases}

A term
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!m
specifies the exchange of message
m
between sender
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and receiver
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
The term represents two local events observed separately due to asynchrony: a send event
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
observed by role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and a receive event
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m
observed by role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
The
+
operator denotes choice,
\mu t.\,G
denotes recursion, and
0
denotes protocol termination.

Fig.

1(a)
visualizes
\mathbf{G}_{oe}
as an HMSC.
The left and right sub-protocols respectively correspond to the top and bottom branches of the protocol.
Role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
chooses a branch by sending either
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
or
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
. On the left,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
echoes this message to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
. Both branches continue in the same way:
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
sends an arbitrary number of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
messages to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, each of which is forwarded twice from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
. Role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
signals the end of the loop by sending
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, which
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
forwards to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
. Finally, depending on the branch,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
must send
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
or
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

Figs.

1(b)
and
1(c)
depict the structural similarity between the global type
\mathbf{G}_{oe}
and the implementations for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
For the “choicemaker” role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, the reason is evident.
Role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
’s implementation collapses the continuations of both branches in the protocol into a single sub-component.
For
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
(
Fig.

1(d)
), the situation is more complicated.
Role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
does not decide on or learn directly which branch is taken, but can deduce it from the parity of the number of
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
messages received from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
: odd means left and even means right.
The resulting local implementation features transitions going back and forth between the two branches that do not exist in the global type.
Syntactic projection operators fail to create such transitions.

\blacktriangleleft

One response to the brittleness of existing projection operators has been to give up on global type specifications altogether and instead revert to model checking user-provided implementations
[
33
,
26
]
.
We posit that what needs rethinking is not the concept of global types, but rather how projections are computed and how implementability is checked.

Our automata-theoretic approach.

The synthesis step in our projection operator uses textbook automata-theoretic constructions.
From a given global type, we derive a finite state machine, and use it to define a homomorphism automaton for each role.
We then determinize
this homomorphism automaton via subset construction to obtain a local candidate implementation for each role.
If the global type is implementable, this construction always yields an implementation.
The implementations shown in
Figs.

1(b)
,
1(c)
, and
1(d)
are the result of applying this construction to
\mathbf{G}_{oe}
from
Example

1
.
Notice that the state labels in
Fig.

1(d)
correspond to sets of labels in the global protocol.

Unfortunately, not all global types are implementable.

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,0\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{r}^{\prime}={}

\displaystyle+\;\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,0\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,0\end{cases}

\displaystyle\hskip 16.38895pt\mathbf{G}_{s}^{\prime}={}

\displaystyle+\;\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,0\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,0\end{cases}

Similar to
\mathbf{G}_{oe}
, in all four examples,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
chooses a branch by sending either
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
or
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
. The global type
\mathbf{G}_{r}
is not implementable because
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
cannot learn which branch was chosen by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
. For any local implementation of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
to be able to execute both branches, it must be able to receive
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
in any order. Because the two send events
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
are independent of each other, they may be reordered. Consequently, any implementation of
\mathbf{G}_{r}
would have to permit executions that are consistent with global behaviors not described by
\mathbf{G}_{r}
, such as
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
. Contrast this with
\mathbf{G}_{r}^{\prime}
, which is implementable. In the top branch of
\mathbf{G}_{r}^{\prime}
, role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can only send to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
after it has received from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
, which prevents the reordering of the send events
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
. The bottom branch is symmetric. Hence,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
learns
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s choice based on which message it receives first.

For the global type
\mathbf{G}_{s}
, role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
again cannot learn the branch chosen by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
. That is,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
cannot know whether to send
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
or
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, leading inevitably to deadlocking executions. In contrast,
\mathbf{G}_{s}^{\prime}
is again implementable because the expected behavior of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
is independent of the choice by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

\blacktriangleleft

These examples show that the implementability question is non-trivial.
To check implementability, we present conditions that precisely characterize when the subset construction for
\mathbf{G}
yields an implementation.

Overview.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
§
3
contains relevant definitions for our work.
§
4
describes the synthesis step of our projection.
§
5
presents the two conditions that characterize implementability of a given global type.
In §
6
, we prove soundness of our projection via a stronger inductive invariant guaranteeing per-role agreement on a global run of the protocol.
In §
7
, we prove completeness by showing that our two conditions hold if a global type is implementable. In §
8
, we discuss the complexity of our construction and condition checks.
§
9
presents our artifact and evaluation, and §
10
as well as §
11
discuss related work.

3
Preliminaries

Words.

Let
\Sigma
be a finite alphabet.

\Sigma^{*}
denotes the set of finite words over
\Sigma
,
\Sigma^{\omega}
the set of infinite words, and
\Sigma^{\infty}\negthinspace
their union
\Sigma^{*}\cup\Sigma^{\omega}
.
A word
u\in\Sigma^{*}
is a
prefix
of word
v\in\Sigma^{\infty}
, denoted
u\leq v
, if there exists
w\in\Sigma^{\infty}
with
u\cdot w=v
.

Message Alphabet.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a set of roles and
\mathcal{V}
be a set of messages. We define the set of
synchronous events

Σ_{\mathit{sync}}\coloneq\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!m\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}∈\mathcal{P}\text{ and }m∈\mathcal{V}\}
where
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!m
denotes that message
m
is sent by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
atomically.
This is split for
asynchronous events
.
For a role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, we define the alphabet

\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P},\;m\in\mathcal{V}\}
of
send
events
and the alphabet

\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},?}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?m\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P},\;m\in\mathcal{V}\}
of
receive
events.
The event
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
denotes role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
sending a message
m
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?m
denotes role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
receiving a message
m
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
We write
\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}\cup\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},?}
,

\Sigma_{!}=\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}
, and

\Sigma_{?}=\bigcup_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},?}
.
Finally,
Σ_{\mathit{async}}=\Sigma_{!}\cup\Sigma_{?}
.
We say that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is
active
in
x\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}
if
x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
For each role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, we define a homomorphism
{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
, where
x{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=x
if
x\in\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
\varepsilon
otherwise.
We write
\mathcal{V}(w)
to project the send and receive events in
w
onto their messages.
We fix
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{V}
in the rest of the paper.

Global Types – Syntax.

Global types for MSTs
[
28
]
are defined by the grammar:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\Coloneqq 0\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.G_{i}\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0pt\mu t.\;G\hskip 3.0pt\mid\hskip 3.0ptt

where
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}
range over
\mathcal{P}
,
m_{i}
over
\mathcal{V}
, and
t
over a set of recursion variables.

We require each branch of a choice to be distinct:

∀i,j∈I.\,i≠j⇒({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i},m_{i})≠({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{j},m_{j})
,
the sender and receiver of an atomic action to be distinct:

∀i∈I.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}
,
and recursion to be guarded:
in
μt.\,G
, there is at least one message between
μt
and each
t
in
G
.
When
|I|=1
, we omit
\sum
.
For readability, we sometimes use the infix operator
+
for choice, instead of
\sum
.
When working with a protocol described by a global type, we write
\mathbf{G}
to refer to the top-level type, and we use
G
to refer to its subterms.
For the size of a global type, we disregard multiple occurrences of the same subterm.

We use the extended definition of global types from
[
28
]
that allows a sender to send messages to different roles in a choice.
We call this
sender-driven choice
, as in
[
36
]
, while it was called generalized choice in
[
28
]
.
This definition subsumes classical MSTs that only allow
directed choice

[
22
]
.
The types we use focus on communication primitives and omit features like delegation or parametrization.
We defer a detailed discussion of different MST frameworks to
Section

11
.

Global Types – Semantics.

As a basis for the semantics of a global type
\mathbf{G}
, we construct a finite state machine

\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})=(Q_{\mathbf{G}},Σ_{\mathit{sync}},δ_{\mathbf{G}},q_{0,\mathbf{G}},F_{\mathbf{G}})

where

•

Q_{\mathbf{G}}
is the set of all syntactic subterms in
\mathbf{G}
together with the term
0
,

•

δ_{\mathbf{G}}
is the smallest set containing

(\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.G_{i},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i},G_{i})
for each
i∈I
,
as well as
(μt.G^{\prime},ε,G^{\prime})
and
(t,ε,μt.G^{\prime})
for each subterm
\mu t.G^{\prime}
,

•

q_{0,\mathbf{G}}=\mathbf{G}
and

F_{\mathbf{G}}=\{0\}
.

We define a homomorphism

\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}
onto the asynchronous alphabet:

\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!m)\coloneq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m\kern 5.0pt.

The semantics
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
of a global type
\mathbf{G}
is given by

\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G}))))
where
\mathcal{C}^{\sim}
is the closure under the indistinguishability relation
\sim

[
28
]
.
Two events are independent if they are not related by the
happened-before
relation
[
24
]
. For instance, any two send events from distinct senders are independent.
Two words are indistinguishable if one can be reordered into the other by repeatedly swapping consecutive independent events. The full definition is in
Section

0.A.2
.

Communicating State Machine
[
10
]
.

\mathcal{A}=\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is a CSM over
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{V}
if

{A}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}

is a finite state machine
over
\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, denoted by

(Q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},q_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},F_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
.
Let

\prod_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}s_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}

denote the set of global states and

\mathsf{Chan}=\{({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\}

denote the set of channels.
A
configuration
of
\mathcal{A}
is a pair
(\vec{s},\xi)
, where
\vec{s}\,
is a global state and

\xi:\mathsf{Chan}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{*}

is a mapping from each channel to a sequence of messages.
We use
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
to denote the state of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
\vec{s}
.
The CSM transition relation, denoted
\rightarrow
, is defined as follows.

•

(\vec{s},\xi)\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m}(\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
if

(\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m,\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,

\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}=\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
for every role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,

\xi^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})=\xi({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})\cdot m
and
\xi^{\prime}(c)=\xi(c)
for every other channel
c\in\mathsf{Chan}
.

•

(\vec{s},\xi)\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m}(\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})
if

(\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m,\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
,

\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}=\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
for every role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
,

\xi({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})=m\cdot\xi^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})

and
\xi^{\prime}(c)=\xi(c)
for every other channel
c\in\mathsf{Chan}
.

In the initial configuration
(\vec{s}_{0},\xi_{0})
, each role’s state in
\vec{s}_{0}
is the initial state
q_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
of
A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and
\xi_{0}
maps each channel to
\varepsilon
.
A configuration
(\vec{s},\xi)
is said to be
final
iff
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is final for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
\xi
maps each channel to
\varepsilon
.
Runs and traces are defined in the expected way.
A run is
maximal
if either it is finite and ends in a final configuration, or it is infinite.
The language
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})
of the CSM
\mathcal{A}
is defined as the set of maximal traces.
A configuration
(\vec{s},\xi)
is a
deadlock
if it is not final and has no outgoing transitions.
A CSM is
deadlock-free
if no reachable configuration is a deadlock.

Finally, implementability is formalized as follows.

Definition 1
(Implementability
[
28
]
)

A global type
\mathbf{G}
is
implementable
if there exists a CSM
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
such that the following two properties hold:

(i)

protocol fidelity
:
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
, and

(ii)

deadlock freedom
:
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deadlock-free.

We say that
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
implements
\mathbf{G}
.

4
Synthesizing Implementations

The construction is carried out in two steps.
First, for each role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, we define an intermediate state machine
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
that is a homomorphism of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
.
We call
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
the
projection by erasure
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, defined below.

Definition 2
(Projection by Erasure)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be some global type
with its state machine

\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})=(Q_{\mathbf{G}},Σ_{\mathit{sync}},\delta_{\mathbf{G}},q_{0,\mathbf{G}},F_{\mathbf{G}})
.
For each role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
, we define the state machine

\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\,=(Q_{\mathbf{G}},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\uplus\{\varepsilon\},\delta_{\downarrow},q_{0,\mathbf{G}},F_{\mathbf{G}})

where

\delta_{\downarrow}\coloneq\{q\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(a){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}q^{\prime}\mid q\xrightarrow{a}q^{\prime}\in\delta_{\mathbf{G}}\}
.
By definition of
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\hbox{-})
, it holds that
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(a){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\in\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\uplus\{\varepsilon\}
.

Then, we determinize
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
via a standard subset construction to obtain a deterministic local state machine for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

Definition 3
(Subset Construction)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a role. Then, the
subset construction
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is defined as

\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})=\bigl(Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}},F_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\bigr)\text{ where }

•

\delta(s,a)\coloneq\{q^{\prime}\in Q_{\mathbf{G}}\mid\exists q\in s,q\xrightarrow{a}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}q^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}\},

for
every

s\subseteq Q_{\mathbf{G}}
and

a\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}

•

s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\coloneq\{q\in Q_{\mathbf{G}}\mid q_{0,\mathbf{G}}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}q\in\delta_{\downarrow}\}
,

•

Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\coloneq\mathrm{lfp}_{\{s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}}^{\subseteq}\lambda Q.\,Q\cup\{\delta(s,a)\mid s\in Q\land a\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}\setminus\{\emptyset\}

, and

•

\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\coloneq{\delta}|_{Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\times\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}

•

F_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\coloneq\{s\in Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\mid s\cap F_{\mathbf{G}}\neq\emptyset\}

Note that the construction ensures that
Q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
only contains subsets of
Q_{\mathbf{G}}
whose states are reachable via the same traces, i.e. we typically have
|Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}|\ll 2^{|Q_{\mathbf{G}}|}
.

The following characterization is immediate from the subset construction; the proof can be found in
Section

0.B.1
.

Lemma 1

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
be a role, and

\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})

be its
subset construction
.
If
w
is a trace of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
,
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(w){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
is a trace of
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
.
If
u
is a trace of
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
, there is a trace
w
of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
such that
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(w){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}=u
.
It holds that

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}=\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}))
.

Using this lemma, we show that the CSM
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
preserves all behaviors of
\mathbf{G}
.

Lemma 2

For all global types
\mathbf{G}
,
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
.

We briefly sketch the proof here.
Given that
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deterministic, to prove language inclusion it suffices to prove the inclusion of the respective prefix sets:

\text{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))\subseteq\text{pref}(\mathcal{L}\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})

Let
w
be a word in
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
.
If
w
is finite, membership in
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
is immediate from the claim above.
If
w
is infinite, we show that
w
has an infinite run in
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
using König’s Lemma.
We construct an infinite graph
\mathcal{G}_{w}(V,E)
with

V\coloneq\{v_{\rho}\mid\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\rho)\leq w\}
and

E\coloneq\{(v_{\rho_{1}},v_{\rho_{2}})\mid\exists~x\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}.~\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\rho_{2})=\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\rho_{1})\cdot x\}
.
Because
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deterministic,
\mathcal{G}_{w}
is a tree rooted at
v_{\varepsilon}
, the vertex corresponding to the empty run.
By König’s Lemma, every infinite tree contains either a vertex of infinite degree or an infinite path.
Because
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
consists of a finite number of communicating state machines, the last configuration of any run has a finite number of next configurations, and
\mathcal{G}_{w}
is finitely branching.
Therefore, there must exist an infinite path in
\mathcal{G}_{w}
representing an infinite run for
w
, and thus

w\in\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
.

The proof of the inclusion of prefix sets proceeds by structural induction and primarily relies on Lemma
1
and the fact that all prefixes in
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
respect the order of send before receive events.

5
Checking Implementability

We now turn our attention to checking implementability of a CSM produced by the subset construction.
We revisit the global types from
Example

2
(also shown in
Fig.

2
), which demonstrate that the naive subset construction does not always yield a sound implementation.
From these examples, we distill our conditions that precisely identify the implementable global types.

In general, a global type
\mathbf{G}
is not implementable when the agreement on a global run of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
among all participating roles cannot be conveyed via sending and receiving messages alone.
When this happens, roles can take locally permitted transitions that commit to incompatible global runs, resulting in a trace that is not specified by
\mathbf{G}
. Consequently, our conditions need to ensure that when a role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
takes a transition in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
, it only commits to global runs that are consistent with the local views of all other roles.
We discuss the relevant conditions imposed on send and receive transitions separately.

Send Validity.

Consider
\mathbf{G}_{s}
from
Example

2
. The CSM

\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G}_{s},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
has an execution with the trace

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
.
This trace is possible because the initial state of
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G}_{s},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
,
s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
, contains two states of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G}_{s})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
, each of which has a single outgoing send transition labeled with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
respectively.
Both of these transitions are always enabled in
s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
, meaning that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
can send
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
even when
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
has chosen the top branch and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
expects to receive
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
instead of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
. This results in a deadlock.
In contrast, while the state
s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G}_{s}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
likewise contains two states of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G}_{s}^{\prime})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
, each with a single outgoing send transition, now both transitions are labeled with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
.
These two transitions collapse to a single one in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G}_{s}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
. This transition is consistent with both possible local views that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
might hold on the global run.

Intuitively, to prevent the emergence of inconsistent local views from send transitions of
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
, we must enforce that for every state
s\in Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
with an outgoing send transition labeled
x
, a transition labeled
x
must be enabled in all states of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
represented by
s
.
We use the following auxiliary definition to formalize this intuition subsequently.

Definition 4
(Transition Origin and Destination)

Let
s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a transition in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
and
\delta_{\downarrow}
be the transition relation of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
We define the set of
transition origins

\operatorname{tr-orig}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})
and
transition destinations

\operatorname{tr-dest}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})
as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr-orig}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\{G\in s\mid\exists G^{\prime}\in s^{\prime}.\,G\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}\}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr-dest}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})\coloneq{}

\displaystyle\{G^{\prime}\in s^{\prime}\mid\exists G\in s.\,G\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G^{\prime}\in\delta_{\downarrow}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Our condition on send transitions is then stated below.

Definition 5
(Send Validity)

\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
satisfies
Send Validity
iff every send transition
s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is enabled in all states contained in
s
:

\forall s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}.~x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}\implies\operatorname{tr-orig}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})=s\kern 5.0pt.

Receive Validity.

To motivate our condition on receive transitions, let us revisit
\mathbf{G}_{r}
from
Example

2
. The CSM
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G}_{r},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
recognizes the following trace not in the global type language
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}_{r})
:

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}\kern 5.0pt.

The issue lies with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
which cannot distinguish between the two branches in
\mathbf{G}_{r}
.
The initial state
s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
of
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G}_{r},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
has two states of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G}_{r})
corresponding to the subterms

G_{t}\coloneq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,0

and

G_{b}\coloneq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,0\,
.
Here,
G_{t}
and
G_{b}
are the top and bottom branch of
\mathbf{G}_{r}
respectively. This means that there are outgoing transitions in
s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
labeled with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
takes the transition labeled
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
, it commits to the bottom branch
G_{b}
.
However, observe that the message
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can also be available at this time point if the other roles follow the top branch
G_{t}
.
This is because
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can send
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
without waiting for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
to first receive from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
In this scenario, the roles disagree on which global run of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G}_{r})
to follow, resulting in the violating trace above.

Contrast this with
\mathbf{G}_{r}^{\prime}
. Here,
s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
again has outgoing transitions labeled with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
. However, if
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
takes the transition labeled
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}
, committing to the bottom branch, no disagreement occurs. This is because if the other roles are following the top branch, then
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is blocked from sending to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
until after it has received confirmation that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
has received its first message from
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.

For a receive transition
s\xrightarrow{x}s_{1}
in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
to be safe, we must enforce that the receive event
x
cannot also be available due to reordered sent messages in the continuation
G_{2}\in s_{2}
of another outgoing receive transition
s\xrightarrow{y}s_{2}
. To formalize this condition, we use the set
M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}
of
available messages
for a syntactic subterm
G
of
\mathbf{G}
and a set of
blocked
roles
\mathcal{B}
. This notion was already defined in
[
28
, Sec. 2.2]
. Intuitively,
M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}
consists of all send events
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}!m
that can occur on the traces of
G
such that

m
will be the first message added to channel
({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
before any of the roles in
\mathcal{B}
takes a step.

Available messages.

The set of available messages is recursively defined on the structure of the global type.
To obtain all possible messages, we need to unfold the distinct recursion variables once.
For this, we define a map
\mathit{get\mu}
from variable to subterms and write
\mathit{get\mu}_{\mathbf{G}}
for
\mathit{get\mu}(\mathbf{G})
:

\mathit{get\mu}(0)\coloneq[\,]

\mathit{get\mu}(t)\coloneq[\,]

\mathit{get\mu}(μt.G)\coloneq[t\mapsto G]∪\mathit{get\mu}(G)

\mathit{get\mu}(\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.G_{i})\coloneq\bigcup_{i∈I}\mathit{get\mu}(G_{i})

The function
M^{\mathcal{B},T}_{(\hbox{-}\ldots)}
keeps a set of unfolded variables
T
, which is empty initially.

M^{\mathcal{B},T}_{(0\ldots)}\coloneq∅\hfill M^{\mathcal{B},T}_{(μt.G\ldots)}\coloneq M^{\mathcal{B},T∪\{t\}}_{(G\ldots)}\hfill M^{\mathcal{B},T}_{(t\ldots)}\coloneq\begin{cases}∅&\text{if}~t∈T\\
M^{\mathcal{B},T∪\{t\}}_{(\mathit{get\mu}_{\mathbf{G}}(t)\ldots)}&\text{if}~t∉T\end{cases}\\
M^{\mathcal{B},T}_{(\sum_{i∈I}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\!:\!m_{i}.G_{i}\ldots)}\coloneq\begin{cases}\bigcup_{i∈I,m∈\mathcal{V}}(M^{\mathcal{B},T}_{(G_{i}\ldots)}\setminus\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m\})∪\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}!m_{i}\}\hskip 9.24994pt\hfill\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}∉\mathcal{B}\\
\bigcup_{i∈I}M^{\mathcal{B}∪\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{i}\},T}_{(G_{i}\ldots)}\hskip 9.24994pt\hfill\text{if}~{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}∈\mathcal{B}\end{cases}

We write
M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}
for
M^{\mathcal{B},\emptyset}_{(G\ldots)}
.
If
\mathcal{B}
is a singleton set, we omit set notation and write
M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}_{(G\ldots)}

for
M^{\{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}}_{(G\ldots)}
.
The set of available messages captures the possible states of all channels before a given receive transition is taken.

Definition 6
(Receive Validity)

\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
satisfies
Receive Validity
iff no receive transition is enabled in an alternative continuation that originates from the same source state:

\begin{array}[]{l}\forall s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}?m_{1}}s_{1},\,s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}?m_{2}}s_{2}\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}.\\
\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}\;\implies\;\forall~G_{2}\in\operatorname{tr-dest}(s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}?m_{2}}s_{2}).\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!m_{1}\notin M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}_{(G_{2}\ldots)}\kern 5.0pt.\end{array}

Subset Projection.

We are now ready to define our projection operator.

Definition 7
(Subset Projection of
\mathbf{G}
)

The
subset projection

\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
of
\mathbf{G}
onto
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
if it satisfies Send Validity and Receive Validity. We lift this operation to a partial function from global types to CSMs in the expected way.

We conclude our discussion with an observation about the syntactic structure of the subset projection: Send Validity implies that no state has both outgoing send and receive transitions (also known as mixed choice).

Corollary 1
(No Mixed Choice)

If
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
satisfies Send Validity, then for all
s\xrightarrow{x_{1}}s_{1},s\xrightarrow{x_{2}}s_{2}\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
x_{1}\in\Sigma_{!}
iff
x_{2}\in\Sigma_{!}
.

6
Soundness

In this section, we prove the soundness of our subset projection, stated as follows.

Theorem 6.1

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type and
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be the subset projection.
Then,
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

implements
\mathbf{G}
.

Recall that implementability is defined as protocol fidelity and deadlock freedom.
Protocol fidelity consists of two language inclusions.
The first inclusion,

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
,
enforces that the subset projection generates at least all behaviors of the global type.
We showed in
Lemma

2
that this holds for the subset construction alone (without Send and Receive Validity).

The second inclusion,

\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
,
enforces that no new behaviors are introduced.
The proof of this direction relies on a stronger inductive invariant that we show for all traces of the subset projection.
As discussed in §
5
, violations of implementability occur when roles commit to global runs that are inconsistent with the local views of other roles.
Our inductive invariant states the exact opposite: that all local views are consistent with one another.
First, we formalize the local view of a role.

Definition 8
(Possible run sets)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type and
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
be the corresponding state machine.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a role and

w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
be a word.
We define the set of possible runs
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)

as all maximal runs of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
that are consistent with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s local view of
w
:

\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)\coloneq\{\rho\text{ is a maximal run of }\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\mid w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\leq\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\rho)){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}\kern 5.0pt.

While
Definition

8
captures the set of maximal runs that are consistent
with the local view of a single role, we would like to refer to the set of runs that is consistent with the local view of all roles. We formalize this as the intersection of the possible run sets for all roles, which we denote as

I(w)\coloneq\bigcap_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)\kern 5.0pt.

With these definitions in hand, we can now formulate our inductive invariant:

Lemma 3

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type and
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be the subset projection.
Let
w
be a trace of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
It holds that

I(w)
is non-empty.

The reasoning for the sufficiency of
Lemma

3
is included in the proof of
Theorem

6.1
, found in
Appendix

0.C
.
In the rest of this section, we focus our efforts on how to show this inductive invariant, namely that the intersection of all roles’ possible run sets is non-empty.

We begin with the observation that the empty trace
\varepsilon
is consistent with all runs.
As a result,

I(\varepsilon)=\bigcap_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(\varepsilon)

contains all maximal runs in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
.
By definition, state machines for global types include at least one run, and the base case is trivially discharged. Intuitively,
I(w)
shrinks as more events are appended to
w
, but we show that at no point does it shrink to
\emptyset
.
We consider the cases where a send or receive event is appended to the trace separately, and show that the intersection set shrinks in a principled way that preserves non-emptiness.
In fact, when a trace is extended with a receive event, Receive Validity guarantees that the intersection set does not shrink at all.

x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
,
w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)
\bigcap_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}(w)
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)
y={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m
,
w^{\prime}=wxu
with
u\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w^{\prime})
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(w^{\prime}y)
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(w^{\prime})
\bigcap_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}(w^{\prime})

Figure 3
:
Evolution of
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{\hbox{-}}(\hbox{-})
sets when
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
sends a message
m
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
receives it.

Lemma 4

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type and
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be the subset projection.
Let
wx
be a trace of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
such that
x\in\Sigma_{?}
.
Then,
I(w)=I(wx)
.

To prove this equality, we further refine our characterization of intersection sets.
In particular, we show that in the receive case, the intersection between the sender and receiver’s possible run sets stays the same, i.e.

\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)\cap\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(w)=\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)\cap\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(wx)\kern 5.0pt.

Note that it is not the case that the receiver only follows a subset of the sender’s possible runs.
In other words,

\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(w)\subseteq\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)

is not inductive.
The equality above simply states that a receive action can only eliminate runs that have already been eliminated by its sender.

Fig.

3
depicts this relation.

Given that the intersection set strictly shrinks, the burden of eliminating runs must then fall upon send events.
We show that send transitions shrink the possible run set of the sender in a way that is
prefix-preserving
.
To make this more precise, we introduce the following definition on runs.

Definition 9
(Unique splitting of a possible run)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
a role, and
w\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
a word. Let
\rho
be a possible run in
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)
.
We define the longest prefix of
\rho
matching
w
:

\alpha^{\prime}\coloneq\max\{\rho^{\prime}\mid\rho^{\prime}\leq\rho~\wedge~\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\rho^{\prime})){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\leq w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}\kern 5.0pt.

If
\alpha^{\prime}\neq\rho
, we can split
\rho
into

\rho=\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta

where
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha\cdot G
,
G^{\prime}
denotes the state following
G
, and
\beta
denotes the suffix of
\rho
following
\alpha\cdot G\cdot G^{\prime}
.
We call
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
the unique splitting of
\rho
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
matching
w
.
We omit the role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
when obvious from context.
This splitting is always unique because the maximal prefix of any
\rho\in\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)
matching
w
is unique.

When role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
fires a send transition
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
,
any run
\rho=\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta

in
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
’s possible run with
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(l){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
is eliminated.
While the resulting possible run set could no longer contain runs that end with
G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
, Send Validity guarantees that it must contain runs that begin with
\alpha\cdot G
.
This is formalized by the following lemma.

Lemma 5

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type and
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be the subset projection.
Let
wx
be a trace of

\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
such that
x\in\Sigma_{!}\cap\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
.
Let
\rho
be a run in
I(w)
, and
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
be the unique splitting of
\rho
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
with respect to
w
.
Then, there exists a run
\rho^{\prime}
in
I(wx)
such that
\alpha\cdot G\leq\rho^{\prime}
.

This concludes our discussion of the send and receive cases in the inductive step to show the non-emptiness of the intersection of all roles’ possible run sets.
The full proofs and additional definitions can be found in
Appendix

0.C
.

7
Completeness

In this section, we prove completeness of our approach.
While soundness states that if a global type’s subset projection is defined, it then implements the global type, completeness considers the reverse direction.

Theorem 7.1
(Completeness)

If
\mathbf{G}
