is implementable, then

\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is defined.

We sketch the proof and refer to
Appendix

0.D
for the full proof.

From the assumption that
\mathbf{G}
is implementable, we know there exists a witness CSM that implements
\mathbf{G}
.
While the soundness proof picks our subset projection as the existential witness for showing implementability – thereby allowing us to reason directly about a particular implementation – completeness only guarantees the existence of some witness CSM.
We cannot assume without loss of generality that this witness CSM is our subset construction; however, we must use the fact that it implements
\mathbf{G}
to show that Send and Receive Validity hold on our subset construction.

We proceed via proof by contradiction: we assume the negation of Send and Receive Validity for the subset construction, and show a contradiction to the fact that this witness CSM implements
\mathbf{G}
.
In particular, we contradict protocol fidelity (
Definition

1
(
i
)), stating that the witness CSM generates precisely the language
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
.
To do so, we exploit a simulation argument: we first show that the negation of Send and Receive Validity forces the subset construction to recognize a trace that is not a prefix of any word in
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
.
Then, we show that this trace must also be recognized by the witness CSM, under the assumption that the witness CSM implements
\mathbf{G}
.

To highlight the constructive nature of our proof, we convert our proof obligation to a witness construction obligation.
To contradict protocol fidelity, it suffices to construct a witness trace
v_{0}
satisfying two properties, where
\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is our witness CSM:

1.

v_{0}
is a trace of
\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, and

2.

the run intersection set of
v_{0}

is empty:

I(v_{0})=\bigcap_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(v_{0})=\emptyset
.

We first establish the sufficiency of conditions
1
and
2
.
Because
\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deadlock-free by assumption, every prefix extends to a maximal trace.
Thus, to prove the inequality of the two languages
\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
, it suffices to prove the inequality of their respective prefix sets. In turn, it suffices to show the existence of a prefix of a word in one language that is not a prefix of any word in the other.
We choose to construct a prefix in the CSM language that is not a prefix in
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
.
We again leverage the definition of intersection sets (
Definition

8
) to weaken the property of language non-membership to the property of having an empty intersection set as follows.
By the semantics of
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
, for any
w\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
, there exists
w^{\prime}\in\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})))
with
w\sim w^{\prime}
.
For any
w^{\prime}\in\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})))
, it trivially holds that
w^{\prime}
has a non-empty intersection set.
Because intersection sets are invariant under the indistinguishability relation
\sim
,
w
must also have a non-empty intersection set.
Since intersection sets are monotonically decreasing, if the intersection set of
w
is non-empty, then for any
v\leq w
, the intersection set of
v
is also non-empty.
Modus tollens of the chain of reasoning above tells us that in order to show a word is not a prefix in
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
, it suffices to show that its intersection set is empty.

Having established the sufficiency of properties
1
and
2
for our witness construction, we present the steps to construct
v_{0}
from the negation of Send and Receive Validity respectively. We start by constructing a trace in
\{\!\!\{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
that satisfies
2
, and then show that
\{\!\!\{B_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
also recognizes the trace, thereby satisfying
1
.
In both cases, let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be the role and
s
be the state for which the respective validity condition is violated.

Send Validity (
Definition

5
).

Let
s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m}s^{\prime}\in\delta_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
be a transition such that

\operatorname{tr-orig}(s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m}s^{\prime})\neq s\kern 5.0pt.

First, we find a trace
u
of
\{\!\!\{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
that satisfies:

(1)

role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is in state
s
in the CSM configuration reached via
u
, and

(2)

the run of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
on
u
visits a state in
s\setminus\operatorname{tr-orig}(s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m}s^{\prime})
.

We obtain such a witness
u
from the
\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(-))
of a run prefix of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
that ends in some state in
s\setminus\operatorname{tr-orig}(s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m}s^{\prime})
.
Any prefix thus obtained satisfies (
1
) by definition of
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
, and satisfies (
2
) by construction.
Due to the fact that send transitions are always enabled in a CSM,
u\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
must also be a trace of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, thus satisfying property
1
by a simulation argument.
We then argue that
u\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
satisfies property
2
, stating that
I(u\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m)
is empty: the negation of Send Validity gives that there exist no run extensions from our candidate state in
s\setminus\operatorname{tr-orig}(s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m}s^{\prime})
with the immediate next action
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}:m
, and therefore there exists no maximal run in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
consistent with
u\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
.

Receive Validity (
Definition

6
).

Let

s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}?m_{1}}s_{1}
and

s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}?m_{2}}s_{2}\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be two transitions,
and let
G_{2}\in\operatorname{tr-dest}(s\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}?m_{2}}s_{2})

such that

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}\text{ and }{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!m_{1}\in M^{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}_{(G_{2}\ldots)}\kern 5.0pt.

Constructing the witness
v_{0}
pivots on finding a trace
u
of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}

such that both

u\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}?m_{1}

and

u\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}?m_{2}

are traces of

\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
Equivalently, we show there exists a reachable configuration of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
in which
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
can receive either message from distinct senders
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}
.
Formally, the local state of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
has two outgoing states labeled with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}?m_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}?m_{2}
, and the channels
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
have
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
at their respective heads.
We construct such a
u
by considering a run in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
that contains two transitions labeled with
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}:m_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}:m_{2}
. Such a run must exist due to the negation of Receive Validity.
We start with the split trace
of this run, and argue that, from the definition of
M(\hbox{-})
and the indistinguishability relation
\sim
, we can perform iterative reorderings using
\sim
to bubble the send action
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!m_{1}
to the position before the receive action
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{2}?m_{2}
.
Then,
1

for
u\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}?m_{1}
holds by a simulation argument.
We then separately show that
2
holds for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}?m_{1}
using similar reasoning as the send case to complete the proof that
u\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}_{1}?m_{1}
suffices as a witness for
v_{0}
.

It is worth noting that the construction of the witness prefix
v_{0}
in the proof immediately yields an algorithm for computing counterexample traces
to implementability
.

Remark 1
(Mixed Choice is Not Needed to Implement Global Types)

Theorem

7.1
basically shows the necessity of Send Validity for implementability.

Corollary

1
shows that Send Validity precludes states with both send and receive outgoing transitions.
Together, this implies that an implementable global type can always be implemented without mixed choice.
Note that the syntactic restrictions on global types do not inherently prevent mixed choice states from arising in a role’s subset construction, as evidenced by
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
in the following type:

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!l.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!m.\,0\;+\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!r.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!m.\,0.

Our completeness result thus implies that this type is not implementable.
Most MST frameworks
[
17
,
22
,
28
]
implicitly force
no mixed choice
through syntactic restrictions on local types.
We are the first to prove that mixed choice states are indeed not necessary for completeness. This is interesting because mixed choice is known to be crucial for the expressive power of the synchronous
\pi
-calculus compared to its asynchronous variant
[
29
]
.

8
Complexity

In this section, we establish that checking implementability for global types is in PSPACE and show that generating an implementation can require exponential time.

Theorem 8.1

The MST implementability problem is in
PSPACE
.

Proof

The decision procedure enumerates for each role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
the subsets of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
This can be done in polynomial space and exponential time.
For each
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
s\subseteq Q_{\mathbf{G}}
, it then

(i)

checks membership of
s
in
Q_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
of
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
, and

(ii)

if
s\in Q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, checks whether all outgoing transitions of
s
in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
satisfy Send and Receive Validity.

Check (
i
) can be reduced to the intersection non-emptiness problem for nondeterministic finite state machines, which is in
PSPACE

[
42
]
.
It is easy to see that check (
ii
) can be done in polynomial time.
In particular, the computation of available messages for Receive Validity only requires a single unfolding of every loop in
\mathbf{G}
.

Note that the synthesis problem has the same complexity.
The subset construction to determinize
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
can be done using a PSPACE transducer.
While the output can be of exponential size, it is written on an extra tape that is not counted towards memory usage.
However, this means we need to perform the validity checks as described above instead of using the computed deterministic state machines.

This result and the fact that local languages are preserved by the subset projection (
Lemma

1
) leads to the following observation.

Corollary 2

Let
\mathbf{G}
be an implementable global type.
Then, the subset projection
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is a local language preserving implementation for
\mathbf{G}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}))=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and can be computed in PSPACE.

While this establishes a PSPACE upper bound, we also show that generating an implementation may require exponential time, even for global types with directed choice.

Lemma 6

Constructing an implementation for a global type with directed choice may require exponential time.

Proof

We construct a family of directed global types
G_{k}
that is implementable and requires a projection of exponential size for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
Hence, constructing the projections requires exponential time in the size of the input.

\displaystyle G_{k}

\displaystyle\coloneq\mu t\,.\,+\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!s\,.\,+\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!a\,.\,t\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!b\,.\,t\end{cases}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!l\,.\,+\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!a\,.\,G_{a,k-1}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!b\,.\,G_{b,k-1}\end{cases}\end{cases}\text{\hskip-27.1249pt}

where

\displaystyle G_{a,i}

\displaystyle\coloneq+\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!a\,.\,G_{a,i-1}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!b\,.\,G_{a,i-1}\end{cases}\;\text{ for }i>0

\displaystyle G_{b,i}

\displaystyle\coloneq+\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!a\,.\,G_{b,i-1}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!b\,.\,G_{b,i-1}\end{cases}\;\text{ for }i>0

\displaystyle G_{a,0}

\displaystyle\coloneq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!d\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!a\,.\,0

\displaystyle G_{b,0}

\displaystyle\coloneq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!d\,.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!:\!b\,.\,0

Intuitively,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
sends a sequence of letters from
\{a,b\}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, followed by
d
to indicate that the sequence is over.
Then,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
needs to send the
k
th last letter back to
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Hence,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
needs to remember the last
k
letters at all times, yielding at least
2^{k}
different states for its projection.
This is a variation of a language that is well-known to be recognisable only by a DFA with exponentially many states, compared to a minimal NFA:
all words over
\{a,b\}
where
a
is the
k
th last letter.
Note that our result does not explicitly require that the implementation is local language preserving but any implementation for
G_{k}
will be by construction.

Table 1
:
Projecting Global Types. For every protocol, we report whether it is implementable
\boldsymbol{\checkmark}
or not
\times
, the time to compute our subset projection and the generalized projection by Majumdar et al.
[
28
]
as well as the outcome as
\boldsymbol{\checkmark}
for “implementable”,
\times
for “not implementable” and (
\times
) for “not known”.
We also give the size of the protocol (number of states and transitions), the number of roles, the combined size of all subset projections (number of states and transitions).

For this, we implemented our subset projection in a prototype tool
[
1
,
35
]
.
It takes a global type as input and computes the subset projection for each role. It was straightforward to implement the core functionality in approximately 700 lines of Python3 code closely following the formalization (E
1
).

We consider global types (and communication protocols) from seven different sources as well as all examples from this work (cf. 1st column of
Table

1
).
Our experiments were run on a computer with an Intel Core i7-1165G7 CPU and used at most 100MB of memory.
The results are summarized in
Table

1
.
The reported size is the number of states and transitions of the respective state machine, which allows not to account for multiple occurrences of the same subterm.
As expected, our tool can project every implementable protocol we have considered (E
2
).

Regarding the comparison against the state of the art (E
3
), we directly compared our subset projection to the incomplete approach by Majumdar et al.
[
28
]
, and found that the run times are in the same order of magnitude in general (typically a few milliseconds).
However, the projection of
[
28
]
fails to project four implementable protocols (including
Example

1
).
We discuss some of the other examples in more detail in the next section.
We further note that most of the run times reported by Scalas and Yoshida
[
33
]
on their model checking based tool are around 1 second and are thus two to three orders of magnitude slower.

10
Discussion

Success of Syntactic Projections Depends on Representation.

Let us illustrate how unfolding recursion helps syntactic projection operators to succeed.
Consider this implementable global type, which is not syntactically projectable:

\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{fold}}\coloneq+\;\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,\mu t_{1}.\,({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,t_{1}\;+\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,0)\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,\mu t_{2}.\,({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,t_{2}\;+\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,0)\end{cases}

.

Similar to projection by erasure, a syntactic projection erases events that a role is not involved in and immediately tries to
merge
different branches.
The merge operator is a partial operator that checks sufficient conditions for implementability.
Here, the merge operator fails for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
because it cannot merge a recursion variable binder and a message reception.
Unfolding the global type preserves the represented protocol and resolves this issue:

\mathbf{G}_{\operatorname{unf}}\coloneq+\;\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,0\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,\mu t_{1}.\,({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,t_{1}\;+\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,0)\end{cases}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,\mu t_{2}.\,({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,t_{2}\;+\;{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,0)\end{cases}

.

(We refer to
Fig.

4
in
Section

0.E.1
for visual representations of both global types.)
This global type can be projected with most syntactic projection operators and shows that the representation of the global type matters for syntactic projectability.
However, such unfolding tricks do not always work, e.g. for the odd-even protocol (
Example

1
).
We avoid this brittleness using automata and separating the synthesis from checking implementability.

Entailed Properties from the Literature.
We defined implementability for a global type as the question of whether there exists a deadlock-free CSM that generates the same language as the global type.
Various other properties of implementations and protocols have been proposed in the literature.
Here, we give a brief overview and defer to
Section

0.E.2
for a detailed analysis.

Progress

[
17
]
, a common property, requires that every sent message is eventually received and every expected message will eventually be sent.
With deadlock freedom, our subset projection trivially satisfies progress for finite traces.
For infinite traces, as expected, fairness assumptions are required to enforce progress.
Similarly, our subset projection prevents
unspecified receptions

[
13
]
and
orphan messages

[
20
,
8
]
, respectively interpreted in our multiparty setting with sender-driven choice.
We also ensure that every local transition of each role is
executable

[
13
]
, i.e. it is taken in some run of the CSM.
Any implementation of a global type has the
stable property

[
26
]
, i.e., one can always reach a configuration with empty channels from every reachable configuration.
While the properties above are naturally satisfied by our subset projection, the following ones can be checked directly on an implementable global type without explicitly constructing the implementation.
A global type is
terminating

[
33
]
iff it does not contain recursion and
never-terminating

[
33
]
iff it does not contain term
0
.

11
Related Work

MSTs were introduced by Honda et al.
[
22
]
with a process algebra semantics, and
the connection to CSMs was established soon afterwards
[
19
]
.

In this work, we present a complete projection procedure for global types with sender-driven choice.
The work by Castagna et al.
[
12
]
is the only one to present a projection that aims for completeness.
Their semantic conditions, however, are not effectively computable and their notion of completeness is “less demanding than the classical ones”
[
12
]
.
They consider multiple implementations, generating different sets of traces, to be sound and complete with regard to a single global type
[
12
, Sec. 5.3]
.
In addition, the algorithmic version of their conditions does not use global information as our message availability analysis does.

MST implementability relates to safe realizability of HMSCs, which is undecidable in general but decidable for certain classes
[
27
]
.
Stutz
[
36
]
showed that implementability of global types that are always able to terminate is decidable.
1
1

1

This syntactic restriction is referred to as 0-reachability in
[
36
]
.

The EXPSPACE decision procedure is obtained via a reduction to safe realizability of globally-cooperative HMSCs, by proving that the HMSC encoding
[
37
]
of any implementable global type is globally-cooperative and generalizing results for infinite executions.
Thus, our PSPACE result both generalizes and tightens the earlier decidability result obtained in
[
36
]
.
Stutz
[
36
]
also investigates how HMSC techniques for safe realizability can be applied to the MST setting – using the formal connection between MST implementability and safe realizability of HMSCs – and establishes an undecidability result for a variant of MST implementability with a relaxed indistinguishability relation.

Similar to the MST setting, there have been approaches in the HMSC literature that tie branching to a role making a choice.
We refer the reader to the work by Majumdar et al.
[
28
]
for a survey.

Standard MST frameworks project a global type to a set of
local types
rather than a CSM.
Local types are easily translated to FSMs
[
28
, Def.11]
.
Our projection operator, though, can yield FSMs that cannot be expressed with the limited syntax of
local
types.
Consider this implementable global type:

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}o}.\,0~+~{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}m}.\,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\!\to\!{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\!:\!{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}.\,0
.
The subset projection for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
has two final states connected by a transition labeled
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
.
In the syntax of local types,
0
is the only term indicating termination, which means that final states with outgoing transitions cannot be expressed.
In contrast to the syntactic restrictions for global types, which are key to effective verification, we consider local types unnecessarily restrictive.
Usually, local implementations are type-checked against their local types and subtyping gives some implementation freedom
[
16
,
15
,
25
,
11
]
.
However, one can also view our subset projection as a local specification of the actual implementation.
We conjecture that subtyping would then amount to a variation of alternating refinement
[
4
]
.

CSMs are Turing-powerful
[
10
]
but decidable classes were obtained for different semantics:
restricted communication topology
[
30
,
40
]
,
half-duplex communication (only for two roles)
[
13
]
,
input-bounded
[
9
]
,
and unreliable channels
[
3
,
2
]
.
Global types (as well choreography automata
[
6
]
) can only express existentially
1
-bounded,
1
-synchronizable and half-duplex communication
[
37
]
.
Key to this result is that sending and receiving a message is specified atomically in a global type – a feature Dagnino et al.
[
18
]
waived for their deconfined global types.
However, Dagnino et al.
[
18
]
use deconfined types to capture the behavior of a given system rather than projecting to obtain a system that generates specified behaviors.

This work relies on reliable communication as is standard for MST frameworks.
Work on fault-tolerant MST frameworks
[
41
,
7
]
attempts to relax this restriction. In the setting of reliable communication, both context-free
[
38
,
23
]
and parametric
[
14
,
21
]
versions of session types have been proposed to capture more expressive protocols and entire protocol families respectively.
Extending our approach to these generalizations is an interesting direction for future work.

This work is funded in part by the National Science Foundation under grant 1815633.
Felix Stutz was supported by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft project 389792660 TRR 248—CPEC.

Appendix 0.A
Additional Material for
Section

3

0.A.1
Additional Definitions

Given a word
w=w_{0}\ldots w_{n}
, we use
w[i]
to denote the i-th symbol
w_{i}\in\Sigma
, and
w[0..i]
to denote the subword between and including
w_{0}
and
w_{i}
,
w_{0}\ldots w_{i}
.

0.A.2
Indistinguishability Relation
[
28
]

We define a family of
indistinguishability relations

{\sim_{i}}\subseteq Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}\times Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
for
i\geq 0

as follows.
For all
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, we have
w\sim_{0}w
.
For
i=1
, we define:

(1)

If
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}
, then

w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}!m^{\prime}.u\;\sim_{1}\;w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}!m^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.u
.

(2)

If
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}
, then

w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m^{\prime}.u\;\sim_{1}\;w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m.u
.

(3)

If
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\land({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}∨{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}≠{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}})
, then

w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m^{\prime}.u\;\sim_{1}\;w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{s}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}?m^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.u
.

(4)

If
\lvert w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!\_}\rvert>\lvert w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?\_}\rvert
,
then

w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m^{\prime}.u\;\sim_{1}\;w.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.u
.

Let
w,w^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime}
be sequences of events s.t.
w\sim_{1}w^{\prime}
and
w^{\prime}\sim_{i}w^{\prime\prime}
for some
i
.
Then,
w\sim_{i+1}w^{\prime\prime}
.
We define
w\sim u
if
w\sim_{n}u
for some
n
.

It is easy to see that
\sim
is an equivalence relation.
Define
u\preceq_{\sim}v
if there is
w\in\Sigma^{*}
such that
u.w\sim v
.
Observe that
u\sim v
iff

u\preceq_{\sim}v
and
v\preceq_{\sim}u
.

For infinite words
u,v\in\Sigma^{\omega}
, we define
u\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}v

if for each finite prefix
u^{\prime}
of
u
, there is a finite prefix
v^{\prime}
of
v
such that

u^{\prime}\preceq_{\sim}v^{\prime}
.
Define
u\sim v
iff
u\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}v
and
v\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}u
.

We lift the equivalence relation
\sim
on
\Sigma^{\infty}
to languages:

\mathcal{C}^{\sim}(L)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{w^{\prime}\mid\bigvee\begin{array}[]{l}w^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{*}\land∃w∈\Sigma^{*}.\;w\in L\text{ and }w^{\prime}\sim w\\
w^{\prime}∈\Sigma^{ω}\land\exists w\in\Sigma^{\omega}.\;w\in L\text{ and }w^{\prime}\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}w\end{array}}}\right\}

For the infinite case, we take the downward closure w.r.t.
\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}
.
Notice that the closure operator is asymmetric.
Consider the protocol
({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m)^{ω}
.
Since we do not make any fairness assumption on scheduling, we need to include in the closure the execution where only the sender is scheduled, i.e.,
({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m)^{ω}\preceq_{\sim}^{\omega}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m.{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m)^{ω}
.

0.A.3
State Machine

A
state machine

A
is a
5
-tuple
(Q,\Delta,\delta,q_{0},F)

where
Q
is a finite set of states,

\Delta
is a finite alphabet,

\delta\subseteq Q\times(\Sigma\cup\{\varepsilon\})\times Q
is a transition relation,

q_{0}\in Q
is the initial state, and

F\subseteq Q
is the set of final states.
As is standard, we write
q\xrightarrow{x}q^{\prime}
for
(q,x,q^{\prime})\in\delta
and
q\xrightarrow{w}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}}^{*}q^{\prime}
for its reflexive and transitive hull for
w\in\Delta^{*}
.
We define the runs and traces in the standard way.
A run is maximal if it is infinite or if it ends at a final state.
The
language

\mathcal{L}(A)
is the set of (finite or infinite) maximal traces.

Appendix 0.B
Additional Material for
Section

4

0.B.1
Proofs for
Section

4

See
1

Proof

All claims are rather straightforward from the definitions and constructions and the proofs exploit the connection to the projection by erasure.
We still spell them out to familiarize the reader with these.

We prove the first claim first.
By construction, for every run
\rho
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
, there exists a run
\rho^{\prime}
in the projection by erasure
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
.
Let
\rho
be the run for trace
w
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
.
Then,
\rho
is also a run in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
with trace
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
Since
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
might be non-deterministic, we apply the subset construction from
Definition

3
.
For the reachable states, this is equivalent to the definition by Sipser
[
34
, Thm. 1.39]
.Thus, the constructed deterministic finite state machine can mimic any run (which is initial by definition) in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
:
for every run
\rho^{\prime}
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
with trace
w^{\prime}
, there is a run
\rho^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
with trace
w^{\prime}
.

For the second claim, we consider a trace
u
of
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
.
Because of the subset construction, it holds that for every run
\rho^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}})
with trace
w^{\prime}
,
there is a run
\rho^{\prime\prime}
in the projection by erasure
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
with trace
w^{\prime}
.
By definition of the projection by erasure,
a run
\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
exists
with the same sequence of syntactic subterms as
\rho^{\prime\prime}
and

\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\rho^{\prime\prime\prime})){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=w^{\prime}
.

From this, it easily follows that

\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}))=\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})\negmedspace\!\downarrow_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}})

and, thus,

\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}))=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}
.

∎

See
2

Proof

Given that
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deterministic, to prove language inclusion it suffices to prove the inclusion of the respective prefix sets:

\text{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))\subseteq\text{pref}(\mathcal{L}\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})

We prove this via structural induction on
w
.
The base case,
w=\varepsilon
, is trivial.
For the inductive step, let

wx\in\text{pref}(\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}))
.
From the induction hypothesis,

w\in\text{pref}(\mathcal{L}\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
.
It suffices to show that the transition labeled with
x
is enabled for the active role in
x
.
Let
(\vec{s},\xi)
denote the
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
configuration reached on
w
.
In the case that
x\in\Sigma_{!}
, let
x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m
.
The existence of an outgoing transition
\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!m}
from
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
follows from the fact that
\mathcal{L}(\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}))=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G}){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
(
Lemma

1
).
The fact that
wx\in\text{pref}(\mathcal{L}\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
follows immediately from this and the fact that send transitions in a CSM are always enabled.
In the case that
x\in\Sigma_{?}
, let
x={\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m
.
We obtain an outgoing transition
\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?m}
from
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
analogously.
We additionally need to show that
\xi({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
contains
m
at the head.
This follows from
[
28
, Lemma 20]
and the induction hypothesis.
This concludes our proof of prefix set inclusion.

Let
w
be a word in
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{G})
.
Let
(\vec{s},\xi)
denote the
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
configuration reached on
w
.
In the case that
w
is finite, all states in
\vec{s}
are final from
Lemma

1
and all channels in
\xi
are empty from the fact that all send events in
w
contain matching receive events.
In the case that
w
is infinite, we show that
w
has an infinite run in
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
using König’s Lemma.
We construct an infinite graph
\mathcal{G}_{w}(V,E)
with

V\coloneq\{v_{\rho}\mid\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\rho)\leq w\}
and

E\coloneq\{(v_{\rho_{1}},v_{\rho_{2}})\mid\exists~x\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}.~\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\rho_{2})=\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\rho_{1})\cdot x\}
.
Because
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
is deterministic,
\mathcal{G}_{w}
is a tree rooted at
v_{\varepsilon}
, the vertex corresponding to the empty run.
By König’s Lemma, every infinite tree contains either a vertex of infinite degree or a ray.
Because
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
consists of a finite number of communicating state machines, the last configuration of any run has a finite number of next configurations, and
\mathcal{G}_{w}
is finitely branching.
Therefore, there must exist a ray in
\mathcal{G}_{w}
representing an infinite run for
w
, and thus

w\in\mathcal{L}(\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}})
.

∎

Appendix 0.C
Additional Material for
Section

6

The definition
M^{\mathcal{B},T}_{(G\ldots)}
computes the set of available messages on the syntax of global types and follows the one by Majumdar et al.
[
28
, Sec. 2.2]
.
For their proofs, they introduce another version, which computes these sets on the semantics of the global type using a concept called blocked languages.
In Lemma 37, they prove the syntactic version always yields a superset of the semantic version.
The proof easily generalizes to equality.
Therefore, we use
M^{\mathcal{B},T}_{(\hbox{-}\ldots)}
in our proofs and refer to their work for details.

Corollary 3
(Intersection sets are invariant under
\sim
)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type.
Let
w,w^{\prime}\in Σ_{\mathit{async}}^{*}
and
w\sim w^{\prime}
. Then,
I(w)=I(w^{\prime})
.

Proof

It follows immediately from
w\sim w^{\prime}
that

\forall{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}.~w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}

By the definition of
I
,

\forall\rho.~\rho\in I(w)\Leftrightarrow\forall{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}.~w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\leq\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\rho)){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}

Let
\rho
be a run in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
. Then,

\displaystyle\rho\in I(w)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}.~w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\leq\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\rho)){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\forall{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}.~w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\leq\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\rho)){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\rho\in I(w^{\prime})

∎

Proposition 1
(Structural properties of
M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}
)

Let
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{P}
and let
G
be a syntactic subterm of
\mathbf{G}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!m\in M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}
.
Then, it holds that:

1.

M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}
does not contain any events whose active role is blocked:

\forall~{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{B}.~M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}\cap\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\emptyset

2.

There exists a run suffix
\beta
such that:

(a)

~G\cdot\beta\text{ is the suffix of a maximal run in }\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
,

(b)

\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}:m}\text{ occurs in }\beta
, and

(c)

\mathcal{B}\text{ monotonically increases during the computation of }M^{\mathcal{B}}_{(G\ldots)}

Proof

Immediate from the definition of available messages in
[
28
, Sec. 2.2]
.

Proposition 2
(Correspondence between unique splittings and local states)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type, and
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be the subset construction for each role. Let
w
be a trace of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, and
(\vec{s},\xi)
be the CSM configuration reached on
w
. Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
be a role.
Then, it holds that:

\bigcup_{\rho\in\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)}\{G\mid\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta\text{ is the unique splitting of }\rho\text{ matching }w\}\subseteq\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}

Proof

Let
\rho
be a run in
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)
, and let
\alpha\cdot G\xrightarrow{l}G^{\prime}\cdot\beta
be its unique splitting for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
matching
w
.
It follows from the definition of unique splitting that

\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\alpha\cdot G^{\prime})){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
From the subset construction, there exists a run
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
in
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
such that
s_{1}=s_{0,{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
and
s_{1}\xrightarrow{w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}s_{n}
.
By the definition of
Q_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, we know that
s_{n}
contains all global syntactic subterms in
\mathbf{G}
that are reachable via
q_{0,\mathbf{G}}\xrightarrow{w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}
in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})_{\downarrow}
, of which
G
is one.
Hence,
G\in s_{n}
.
By assumption,
\mathscr{C}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
reached state
\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
on
w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}
.
Because subset constructions are deterministic, it follows that
s_{n}=\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
We conclude that
G\in\vec{s}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.

∎

Proposition 3
(No send transitions from final states in subset projection)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type, and
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
be the subset projection for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Let
s\in F_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
, and
s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}\in\delta_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Then,
x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},?}
.

Proof

Assume by contradiction that
x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}
.
We instantiate Send Validity with
s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime}
to obtain:

x\in\Sigma_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},!}\implies\operatorname{tr-orig}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})=s

By the definition of
\operatorname{tr-orig}(\hbox{-})
, for every syntactic subterm
G\in\operatorname{tr-orig}(s\xrightarrow{x}s^{\prime})
:

\exists G^{\prime}\in s^{\prime}.~G\xrightarrow{x}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}G^{\prime}\in\delta{\upharpoonright}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}

Because
s
is a final state in
\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
, by definition it must contain a syntactic subterm that is a final state in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G}){\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
.
Because
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G}){\upharpoonright}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
share the same set of final states
2
,
s
must contain a syntactic subterm that is a final state in
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
.
Let
G_{0}
denote this final state.
By the structure of
\mathsf{GAut}(\mathbf{G})
, there exists no outgoing transition from
G_{0}
.
Therefore,
G^{\prime}
does not exist.
We reach a contradiction.

∎

Definition 10
(
\mathbf{G}
-complete words of
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
)

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a global type,
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
be a CSM, and
w
be a trace of
\{\!\!\{A_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
.
We say
w
is
\mathbf{G}
-complete
if for all roles
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and for all runs
\rho\in\bigcap_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)
,

w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}=\bigl(\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\rho))\bigr){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}.

Definition 11
(Channel-compliant
[
28
]
)

A word
w\in\Sigma^{\infty}
is channel-compliant if
for every prefix
w^{\prime}\leq w
and every
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\in\mathcal{P}
,

\mathcal{V}(w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}?\_})\leq\mathcal{V}(w^{\prime}{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}!\_})
.

See
4

Proof

Let
x
=
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?m
.
Because
wx
is a trace of
\{\!\!\{{\mathscr{P}(\mathbf{G},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})}\}\!\!\}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}
, there exists a run

(\vec{s}_{0},\xi_{0})\xrightarrow{w}\mathrel{\vphantom{\to}{}^{*}}(\vec{s},\xi)\xrightarrow{x}(\vec{s}\mkern 2.0mu\vphantom{s}^{\prime},\xi^{\prime})

such that

m
is at the head of
\xi({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}})
.

We assume that
I(w)
is non-empty; if
I(w)
is empty then
I(wx)
is trivially empty.
To show
I(w)=I(wx)
, it suffices to show the following claim.

Claim 1 :

It holds that

\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)\cap\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(w)=\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)\cap\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(wx)
.

We first show Claim 1’s sufficiency for
I(w)=I(wx)
:
By definition,

I(w)=\bigcap_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}(w)\subseteq\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(w)\cap\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)
.
With
Claim 1
, it holds that

I(w)\subseteq\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(wx)\cap\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)
.
From this, it follows that

I(w)\subseteq\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)
(H1).
Since
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
is the active role for the receive event
x
,
i.e.
x\in\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
it holds for any
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
,
that

(wx){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}=w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}

and

\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}(w)=\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}(wx)
(H2).
Again, by definition of
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{\hbox{-}}(\hbox{-})
, it holds that

\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)\subseteq\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)

(H3).

We apply the observations to
I(wx)
:

\displaystyle I(wx)

\displaystyle\overset{\phantom{\text{(H2)}}}{=}\bigcap_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}(wx)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(H2)}}{=}\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)\;\cap\bigcap_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{P}\land{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}(w)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(H3)}}{=}\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)\;\cap\;\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)\;\cap\;\bigcap_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\in\mathcal{P}\land{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{r}}}}}(w)

\displaystyle\overset{\phantom{\text{(H3)}}}{=}\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)\;\cap\;I(w)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{(H1)}}{=}I(w)\kern 5.0pt.

Proof of Claim 1:

We instantiate (H2) for role
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
, which yields

\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(wx)=\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(w)
.
The proof of Claim 1 therefore amounts to showing:

\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)\cap\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(w)=\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)\cap\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(w)\kern 5.0pt.

The right direction,
i.e.,
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)\subseteq\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)
,
follows from (H3).
For the left direction,
i.e.,
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)\cap\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(w)\subseteq\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)
,
assume by contradiction that there exists a run
\rho_{0}
such that

\rho_{0}\in\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)\;\land\;\rho_{0}\in\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}}(w)\;\land\;\rho_{0}\notin\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)\kern 5.0pt.

Let
\rho^{\prime}
be a run in
\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(w)\setminus\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)
.
Let
\alpha^{\prime}\cdot G^{\prime}_{\mathit{pre}}\xrightarrow{l^{\prime}}G^{\prime}_{\mathit{post}}\cdot\beta^{\prime}
be the unique splitting of
\rho^{\prime}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
matching
w
.

Let
\rho^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
denote the largest consistent prefix of
\rho^{\prime}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
; it is clear that

\rho^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=\alpha^{\prime}\cdot G^{\prime}_{pre}
. Formally,

\rho^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}=max\{\rho~|~\rho\leq\rho^{\prime}~\land~\bigl(\operatorname{\texttt{{split}}}(\operatorname{\texttt{{trace}}}(\rho))\bigr){\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\leq w{\Downarrow}_{\Sigma_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}\}\kern 5.0pt.

Let
\rho^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
be defined analogously.

We claim that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
is ahead of
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
in
\rho^{\prime}
, i.e.
\rho^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}<\rho^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
Intuitively, this claim follows from the half-duplex property of CSMs and the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
is the sender.
Formally, Lemma 19 in
[
28
]
implies
\xi({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})=u
where

\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!\_})=\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?\_}).u
.
Because
\xi({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}},{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}})
contains at least
m
by assumption,

|\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!\_})|>|\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?\_})|
.
Because

\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?\_})<\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}!\_})

and traces of CSMs are channel-compliant (Lemma 19,
[
28
]
), it holds that

\rho^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
contains all

|\mathcal{V}(w{\Downarrow}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}\triangleleft{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}?\_})|

transition labels of the form

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}:\hbox{-}

that are contained in
\rho^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
,
plus at least one more of the form

{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}:m
.
Because both
\rho^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
and
\rho^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
are prefixes of
\rho^{\prime}
,
it must be the case that

\rho^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}<\rho^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}
.
This concludes the proof of the above claim.

By assumption,
\rho^{\prime}\notin\operatorname{R}^{\mathbf{G}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}}(wx)
and therefore
l^{\prime}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{q}}}}\xrightarrow{}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}:m
.
By the definition of unique splittings,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
must be the active role in
l^{\prime}
; by
Corollary

1
,
{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.3,0.1}\mathtt{p}}}}
must be the receiving role in
l^{\prime}
.
In other words,
l^{\prime}
must be of the form
